```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Partial shape preservation: mechanistic hypothesis.}
5	Near-stationary features (entropy\_max, tail\_decay) remain stable across generation depths ($|\rho| < 0.05$), while drifting features shift monotonically (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
6	Entropy\_std shows moderate pooled correlation ($|\rho| = 0.200$), intermediate between the strongly drifting central-tendency features and the near-stationary upper-tail features.
7	The refined pattern is \emph{lower-tail compression with upper-tail preservation}: entropy\_min (the NLL floor) decreases with depth ($\rho = -0.406$; per-generator $|\rho|$ up to 0.51), indicating that the model's most confident predictions become progressively more confident under recursion; meanwhile entropy\_max (the NLL ceiling) is unchanged ($|\rho| = 0.050$).
8	Mechanistically, recursive generation compresses the lower tail of the NLL distribution while preserving upper-tail behavior, resulting in a net location shift with partial shape preservation.
9	The softmax and top-$p$ sampler impose an effective ceiling on per-token surprise, explaining upper-tail invariance; but conditioning on progressively more predictable context allows the model to produce increasingly confident (low-NLL) tokens, compressing the lower tail.
10	Cross-generator pooling produces minimal attenuation for entropy\_std (per-generator median $|\rho| = 0.209$, pooled $|\rho| = 0.200$), indicating consistent moderate depth sensitivity across generators.
11	This predicts that upper-tail features should remain approximately stationary until generation depth reaches the model's fixed-point distribution, a regime not yet observed in our chain lengths ($\leq$4 steps).
12	Sampling ablation is consistent with this account: depth signal is robust to moderate temperature variation (${\leq}0.3$\,pp for $t \in [0.5, 0.7]$) but degrades substantially at $t = 1.0$ ($-6$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}), where the sampling mechanism itself becomes the dominant source of distributional shape variation, overwhelming the depth signal.
13	
14	\paragraph{Comparison with detector ensembles.}
15	In binary evaluation (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+), our 7-feature ensemble (F1\,=\,0.723) performs comparably to a 4-detector+surface ensemble (0.724) combining four pretrained detection scores with two surface statistics.
16	We do not claim feature-engineering superiority: the primary contributions are the task formulation (ordinal depth estimation beyond binary detection), the empirical characterization of differential depth response, and interpretability---seven summary statistics from a single lightweight reference model versus four independent detection pipelines.
17	That both ensembles converge on near-identical binary performance suggests depth signal is accessible through multiple feature families; our approach offers a simpler, more interpretable route to the same discriminative boundary.
18	
19	\paragraph{Reference model choice.}
20	Qwen3-0.6B serves dual roles as both generator and cross-family reference model in Table~\ref{tab:cross-ref}, creating a potential self-referencing bias.
21	The measured advantage is modest: Qwen3-0.6B reference achieves LOGO F1\,=\,0.816 versus 0.803 for Pythia-410M (+1.3\,pp), within the range of inter-reference variation (spread 0.013).
22	All main results use Pythia-410M as the default reference to avoid this overlap; the cross-reference experiment (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) serves solely to confirm that differential depth response is reference-model-independent.
23	
24	\paragraph{Prompt-type dependence.}
25	Differential depth response strength varies substantially across prompt types.
26	Continuation chains exhibit 16.9\% depth retention loss by gen-4, while elaboration chains retain 98.6\% of samples (Appendix~\ref{app:prompt-type}).
27	The 7-feature ensemble achieves within-type CV F1 of 0.781 for continuation vs.\ 0.730 for elaboration; cross-prompt-type transfer (LOPTO F1\,=\,0.753) confirms shared signal despite differing drift magnitudes.
28	Together with the paraphrastic attenuation reported below (binary F1\,=\,0.671 vs.\ 0.749 for continuation), these results bound depth fingerprinting's primary applicability to continuation-style generation, where distributional drift is strongest; near-paraphrastic settings approach the method's detection floor as semantic-preservation constraints anchor distributional shift \cite{broken_telephone}.
29	
30	\paragraph{Corpus-level vs.\ document-level utility.}
31	The practical value of depth estimation depends on the unit of analysis.
32	At the individual document level, gen-1 per-class F1\,=\,0.64 implies a 36\% misclassification rate---insufficient for automated filtering but suitable for flagging suspect documents for human review.
33	At the corpus level, aggregate depth composition can be estimated with substantially lower error: over $n$ documents, estimation error scales as $O(1/\sqrt{n})$---analogous to opinion polling, where individual voter prediction is unreliable but population-level estimates are actionable---making corpus-level provenance characterization reliable even with moderate per-document accuracy.
34	The 3-class design explicitly targets the most informative boundaries: human vs.\ machine (gen-0 vs.\ gen-1+) and shallow vs.\ deep recursion (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+), where the distributional distinction is most actionable.
35	
36	\paragraph{Relation to model collapse.}
37	Our results relate to, but are distinct from, model collapse \cite{curse_of_recursion,strong_model_collapse,self_consuming_mad}.
38	In training-loop collapse, gradient updates on self-generated data cause progressive mode collapse---catastrophic diversity loss after 5--10 retraining generations \cite{curse_of_recursion}.
39	Our inference-chain setting involves no parameter updates; drift instead accumulates through repeated sampling from the model's high-probability regions, yielding a milder effect (hapax ratio declines ${\sim}$14\% over 4 depths).
40	Both processes share declining diversity, but they diverge in distributional shape: training collapse alters the distribution's functional form as learned parameters shift, whereas inference chains exhibit partial shape preservation---upper-tail features remain stationary because the decoding mechanism remains fixed, though variance (entropy\_std) shows moderate drift.
41	Speculatively, these trends suggest that surface features may serve as leading indicators of deeper distributional shifts, though this hypothesis requires longitudinal validation.
42	Topic drift mediates rather than confounds this process: semantic narrowing accounts for less than 9\% of feature variance (maximum $R^2 = 0.082$ for entropy\_mean), and shape features are near-independent of semantic drift ($|\rho| = 0.068$).
43	
44	\paragraph{Granularity ceiling.}
45	Five-class depth estimation reduces LOGO F1 from 0.789 to 0.501; gen-2 and gen-3 are inseparable (per-class F1\,$\approx$\,0.26), establishing three classes as the practical ceiling for handcrafted features (Appendix~\ref{app:finer-depth}).
46	QLoRA fine-tuning raises 3-class F1 to 0.905 (+11.6\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:qlora}), confirming exploitable depth signal beyond summary statistics.
47	
48	\paragraph{Robustness to post-generation editing.}
49	Depth features degrade gracefully under simulated editing: 20\% random token replacement reduces 3-class F1 by only 6.0\,pp, while LLM rephrasing drops binary F1 by 6.3\,pp (Appendix~\ref{app:human-edit}).
50	This asymmetry---surface noise is tolerable, systematic distributional manipulation is not---is consistent with depth signal residing in aggregate distributional properties rather than individual token choices.
51	
52	\paragraph{Paraphrastic chains.}
53	Initial paraphrastic experiments exhibited reversed entropy--depth correlations ($\rho = +0.394$), but removing contaminated samples aligned correlations with continuation chains ($\rho = -0.533$), confirming the reversal was an artifact.
54	On clean data, paraphrastic chains achieve binary F1\,=\,0.671 (vs.\ 0.749 for continuation), consistent with faster convergence under semantic-preservation constraints \cite{broken_telephone}.
55	Cross-transfer is effective (para$\to$cont F1\,=\,0.770, cont$\to$para 0.743), indicating shared depth signal.
56	
57	\paragraph{Instruct tuning.}
58	Instruction-tuned models show substantially weaker depth fingerprints (OLMo-Instruct LOGO F1\,=\,0.338 vs.\ 0.764 base; Qwen3-0.6B-Instruct 0.667 vs.\ 0.816 base under the 7-generator split; base-only yields 0.824, Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}).
59	Length-matched controls reduce correlations to near zero (all $|\rho| < 0.02$ for Qwen3-0.6B), and binary classification is near chance (F1\,$\approx$\,0.5), indicating that depth signal is not operationally detectable in instruct chains.
60	This justifies our restriction to base models: instruction tuning introduces distributional effects (output length regulation, format constraints) that dominate the subtler recursive drift signal, making base models the appropriate setting for isolating the generation mechanism.
61	
62	\paragraph{Negative downstream finding.}
63	Depth-aware curation does not improve downstream language model quality over random subsampling (\S\ref{sec:results-downstream}).
64	This negative result carries positive information value: it rules out depth-based filtering as a data selection strategy, sparing practitioners from building depth-conditional pipelines that would not yield quality gains.
65	The dominant factor is data quantity, not depth composition---consistent with findings emphasizing data volume over curation sophistication \cite{self_consuming_mad}.
66	
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Web-crawled corpora increasingly contain LLM-generated text---estimates suggest that 7--17\% of recent scientific papers and peer reviews contain substantial LLM-modified content \cite{mapping_llm_science,monitoring_ai_review}---and this proportion grows as generated text re-enters the web and seeds subsequent generation.
5	The result is recursive generation chains whose outputs progressively diverge from human-written text.
6	Understanding the distributional properties of such recursive generation is a prerequisite for characterizing text provenance, yet the phenomenon itself---how distributional features evolve across generation depths---remains underexplored.
7	
8	We study distributional drift in non-paraphrastic generation chains under controlled conditions, using six base (pretrained) models from three families (Pythia, OLMo, Qwen) spanning multiple scales.
9	We restrict to base models as a deliberate design choice: instruction-tuning introduces confounds---output length variability, format constraints, safety-driven distribution shaping---that obscure the generation mechanism's intrinsic behavior.
10	Preliminary experiments confirm this concern: instruct models show substantially degraded depth signal (\S\ref{sec:discussion}), motivating the controlled base-model setting as the appropriate starting point for isolating recursive drift phenomena.
11	
12	Existing detectors \cite{fast_detectgpt,binoculars,ld_score,detectgpt}, like classical out-of-distribution methods \cite{ood_baseline,energy_ood,mahalanobis_ood}, provide binary real-vs-synthetic classification, conflating qualitatively different material.
13	A first-generation continuation retains much of the source distribution's lexical and structural properties; deeper recursive text exhibits measurably reduced vocabulary diversity and compressed perplexity.
14	We formulate \emph{ordinal depth estimation}---classifying text into generation depth classes (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+) rather than producing a binary label---and refer to the overall framework as \emph{depth fingerprinting}.
15	No existing method addresses this task in non-paraphrastic continuation chains.
16	When repurposed as ordinal depth predictors, individual detection scores exhibit high cross-generator variance (binary F1 from 0.52 to 0.71; \S\ref{sec:results-baselines}), making any single detector unreliable.
17	Concurrent work on iterative paraphrasing chains \cite{broken_telephone,markovian_chains} analyzes convergence under semantic-preservation constraints; we study non-paraphrastic chains where the absence of semantic anchoring permits richer depth signal.
18	
19	We characterize a \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency and diversity features---including reference-model mean entropy, type-token ratio, and hapax ratio---drift monotonically with generation depth, while upper-tail features (entropy max, tail decay) remain near-stationary.
20	We hypothesize that recursive generation induces a location shift in the NLL distribution with partial shape preservation; under this account, the softmax ceiling constrains upper-tail behavior regardless of input content, while variance shows moderate drift.
21	The combination of drifting and stationary features yields a 7-feature ensemble that achieves binary F1\,=\,0.723 for leak-free depth discrimination (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+).
22	However, the strength of this signal is prompt-type dependent: continuation chains exhibit stronger drift than elaboration or rewriting chains (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
23	
24	We additionally report a negative finding: despite the effectiveness of quality-based data curation for pretraining \cite{fineweb,dolma}, depth-aware curation does not improve downstream language model quality compared to random subsampling at equal sample count (perplexity: 2.367 vs.\ 2.387).
25	
26	Our contributions are:
27	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
28	    \item \textbf{Task formulation.} We define ordinal generation depth estimation as a task beyond binary detection, providing a framework for characterizing how distributional properties evolve across recursive generation depths.
29	    \item \textbf{Empirical characterization.} We document differential depth response across six base-model generators spanning three model families: a 7-feature ensemble combining drifting and stationary features achieves binary F1\,=\,0.723 for leak-free depth discrimination, outperforming all individual detection baselines ($p < 0.001$).
30	\end{enumerate}
31	
32	The remainder of this paper is organized as follows.
33	\S\ref{sec:related} surveys related work on text detection, model collapse, and data quality.
34	\S\ref{sec:method} describes generation chain construction, feature extraction, and evaluation protocol.
35	\S\ref{sec:results} presents classification results, differential depth response analysis, and baseline comparisons.
36	\S\ref{sec:discussion} discusses mechanistic hypotheses and scope boundaries.
37	
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We introduced ordinal generation depth estimation as a task for characterizing how distributional properties evolve across recursive LLM generation.
5	In a controlled study of six base-model generators from three families (0.6B--8.2B parameters), we documented differential depth response: central-tendency features drift monotonically while upper-tail features remain near-stationary, consistent with recursive generation inducing a location shift in the NLL distribution with partial shape preservation.
6	A 7-feature ensemble achieves binary F1\,=\,0.723 for leak-free depth discrimination (gen-1 vs.\ gen-2+), and cross-model chains confirm signal persistence under generator heterogeneity (3-class F1\,=\,0.809, binary F1\,=\,0.764).
7	Depth-aware data curation does not improve downstream language model quality, indicating that depth composition per se does not determine training utility.
8	
9	These results establish depth fingerprinting as a tool for understanding recursive generation phenomena in base models.
10	Extending to instruction-tuned models---where depth signal degrades substantially---and frontier-scale generators (70B+) are priorities for future work.
11	All code, data, and trained models will be released upon publication to facilitate reproducibility.
12	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01BJh9QQPa4GmpsVr4dvpLVp`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Task Formulation}
5	\label{sec:task}
6	
7	Given a text $x$, the goal is to predict its \emph{generation depth} $d \in \{0, 1, 2{+}\}$, where $d{=}0$ denotes human-written text, $d{=}1$ denotes first-generation LLM output conditioned on human text, and $d{=}2{+}$ denotes text produced by recursively conditioning an LLM on its own prior output at least twice.
8	Formally, let $\mathcal{C}_0$ be a reference corpus of human-written text.
9	A generation chain of depth $k$ is defined recursively: $x^{(0)} \sim \mathcal{C}_0$, and $x^{(k)} = G(x^{(k-1)})$ for $k \geq 1$, where $G$ denotes stochastic LLM generation conditioned on its input.
10	We frame depth estimation as an ordinal classification task over three classes rather than binary real-vs-synthetic detection, enabling finer-grained provenance characterization.
11	Depths beyond 2 are merged into a single $2{+}$ class, consistent with monotonic feature convergence that compresses inter-class margins beyond depth 2 (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
12	
13	\subsection{Generation Chain Construction}
14	\label{sec:generation}
15	
16	We construct generation chains using six generators spanning three model families: \textbf{Pythia} (2.8B, 1B) \cite{pythia}, \textbf{OLMo} (OLMo-2-1B) \cite{olmo}, and \textbf{Qwen} (Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B-Base) \cite{qwen3}.
17	This selection covers parameter scales from 0.6B to 8.2B and diverse pretraining corpora, enabling evaluation of cross-generator and cross-scale generalization.
18	
19	For each generator, we sample approximately 1{,}000 seed passages from English Wikipedia as $\mathcal{C}_0$.
20	The same pool of seed passages serves as gen-0 for all generators.
21	We use shared seeds across generators to ensure that cross-generator comparisons control for input content, isolating the effect of the generation model from source text variation.
22	In LOGO evaluation, gen-0 samples from the held-out generator's chains also appear (with the same text) in the training generators' chains, creating a potential train--test overlap for the gen-0 class.
23	We address this with a supplementary binary evaluation excluding gen-0 entirely (\S\ref{sec:results-baselines}).
24	Each seed is iteratively fed through the generator to produce a chain of depth 4 (i.e., gen-0 through gen-4), using the prompt ``\texttt{Continue the following text: \{text\}}''.
25	All generation uses temperature $\tau{=}0.7$, nucleus sampling with $p{=}0.9$, and maximum length of 256 new tokens.
26	The theoretical yield is $6 \times {\sim}1{,}000 \times 5 \approx 30{,}000$ texts; after removing generation failures, exact duplicates, and passages shorter than 50 tokens, 27{,}529 texts remain.
27	For classification, depths 2--4 are collapsed into the $2{+}$ class.
28	
29	\subsection{Feature Extraction}
30	\label{sec:features}
31	
32	The seven features are motivated by two complementary views of distributional drift.
33	Reference-model features capture how the text's token-level surprise distribution shifts relative to a fixed model; surface features capture how lexical diversity contracts under recursive generation.
34	Together, they span both the generative mechanism (what the model produces) and the surface manifestation (how the vocabulary changes).
35	This two-pronged design is not post-hoc: it follows directly from the hypothesis that recursive generation induces a location shift in the NLL distribution (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}).
36	
37	We extract seven distributional features per text, organized into two groups based on what they measure rather than how they are computed.
38	
39	\paragraph{Reference-model features.}
40	A held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.
41	Although Pythia-410M shares the GPT-NeoX architecture with two of the six generators, cross-family reference model experiments (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) confirm that depth features are not reference-specific (LOGO F1 varies by only 0.013 across Pythia, OLMo, and Qwen references).
42	From the resulting NLL sequence, we extract five statistics:
43	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
44	    \item \textbf{entropy\_mean}\footnote{We use ``entropy'' as shorthand for token-level negative log-likelihood under the reference model, following convention in prior detection work. These are point-wise NLL values, not Shannon entropy of the full distribution.}: mean token-level NLL, capturing the average predictability of the text under the reference model;
45	    \item \textbf{entropy\_std}: standard deviation of token-level NLL, measuring the spread of per-token surprise;
46	    \item \textbf{entropy\_max}: maximum token-level NLL, capturing the most surprising token;
47	    \item \textbf{entropy\_min}: minimum token-level NLL, capturing the most predictable token---as generation depth increases, the floor of token-level surprise drops, reflecting the model's increasing confidence on its own outputs.
48	    Although entropy\_min shows near-zero marginal impact in leave-one-feature-out analysis ($\Delta$F1\,=\,+0.01\,pp), we retain it for completeness of the NLL summary statistics (mean, std, max, min) and because removing it does not improve performance;
49	    \item \textbf{tail\_decay}: proportion of tokens whose NLL exceeds the 90th percentile of the reference distribution (computed over $\mathcal{C}_0$), measuring how heavy-tailed the NLL distribution is relative to human text.
50	    Sensitivity analysis confirms stable classification (F1 variation ${<}$0.3\,pp) across thresholds from the 85th to 95th percentile.
51	\end{itemize}
52	
53	\paragraph{Surface features.}
54	Two features capture lexical diversity without requiring a reference model:
55	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
56	    \item \textbf{TTR}: type-token ratio (number of unique word types divided by total tokens);
57	    \item \textbf{hapax\_ratio}: proportion of words appearing exactly once (\emph{hapax legomena}).
58	\end{itemize}
59	
60	\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}
61	The grouping reflects an empirical pattern we term \emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency, diversity, and lower-bound features (entropy\_mean, TTR, hapax\_ratio, entropy\_min) drift monotonically with generation depth ($|\rho| = 0.20$--$0.65$); upper-tail features (entropy\_max, tail\_decay) remain near-stationary ($|\rho| < 0.05$); and entropy\_std occupies an intermediate position ($|\rho| = 0.200$).
62	This differential behavior---consistent with recursive generation compressing the lower tail of the NLL distribution while preserving upper-tail behavior---motivates combining both feature types.
63	As shown in \S\ref{sec:results}, the full 7-feature set significantly outperforms either group alone.
64	
65	\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}
66	\label{sec:evaluation}
67	
68	\paragraph{Cross-validation.}
69	To evaluate generalization to unseen generators, we use \textbf{Leave-One-Generator-Out (LOGO)} cross-validation: in each fold, one of the six generators is held out for testing and the remaining five provide training data.
70	We additionally report \textbf{Leave-One-Family-Out (LOFO)} results, where an entire model family (e.g., all Qwen models) is held out, testing generalization across architectural lineages.
71	
72	\paragraph{Classifier.}
73	We use XGBoost \cite{xgboost} as the classifier throughout.
74	Gradient-boosted trees handle heterogeneous feature scales and nonlinear interactions without extensive hyperparameter tuning, and their feature importance scores support interpretability.
75	We use $n\_\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\max\_\text{depth}{=}6$, and learning rate $0.1$ for the 7-feature ensemble.
76	Baseline detectors (\S\ref{sec:results-baselines}) use the same XGBoost framework with $n\_\text{estimators}{=}100$ and $\max\_\text{depth}{=}3$ to avoid overfitting their single-feature input.
77	LOGO F1 is stable across random seeds (mean\,=\,0.789, std\,=\,0.0009 over 5 seeds), confirming that results are not sensitive to classifier initialization.
78	Alternative classifiers (random forest, logistic regression) yield comparable performance (Appendix~\ref{app:classifier}), confirming that discriminative power resides in the features, not the classifier.
79	We evaluated two ordinal-specific methods (CORAL \cite{coral_ordinal}, Frank-Hall ordinal ensemble \cite{frank_hall_ordinal}); neither significantly outperforms multiclass XGBoost (3-class LOGO F1 0.771--0.793 vs.\ 0.789, CIs overlapping), confirming that with only three ordinal levels, ordinal structure provides minimal additional benefit.
80	
81	\paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}
82	The primary metric is \textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes, which weights minority classes (gen-1, gen-2+) equally against the typically easier gen-0 class.
83	We report per-class F1 scores and 95\% confidence intervals via \emph{percentile bootstrap} ($n{=}1{,}000$), resampling at the document level within each held-out fold.
84	For pairwise model comparisons (e.g., full features vs.\ ablated subsets), we use the \textbf{McNemar test} on disagreement tables.
85	For comparisons against detection baselines, we use \textbf{paired bootstrap} significance tests.
86	All reported $p$-values are two-sided.
87	We report six pairwise comparisons in \S\ref{sec:results-baselines}; applying Bonferroni correction ($\alpha_{\text{adj}} = 0.05/6 = 0.0083$), all remain significant at $p < 0.001$.
88	Feature--depth correlations (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) are computed on class-balanced subsamples (equal samples per depth class) to control for depth-class imbalance in the generation pipeline.
89	
90	\paragraph{Baselines.}
91	We compare the 7-feature ensemble against four single-score detection methods repurposed as ordinal depth predictors: Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT \cite{fast_detectgpt}, Binoculars \cite{binoculars}, and LD-Score \cite{ld_score}.
92	Each baseline score is used as a univariate feature in the same XGBoost framework under LOGO cross-validation, ensuring a controlled comparison.
93	
94	\paragraph{Length confound control.}
95	Since TTR and hapax\_ratio are mechanically length-dependent, we verify that depth signal is not an artifact of systematic length variation.
96	We conduct three complementary analyses: (1) Spearman correlation between text length and each feature (requiring $|\rho| < 0.15$ for non-confounded features), (2) classification with length-normalized features, and (3) comparison on length-matched subsets.
97	
```

2{+}\\}$, where $d{=}0$ denotes human-written text, $d{=}1$ denotes first-generation LLM output conditioned on human text, and $d{=}2{+}$ denotes text produced by recursively conditioning an LLM on its own prior output at least twice.\nFormally, let $\\mathcal{C}_0$ be a reference corpus of human-written text.\nA generation chain of depth $k$ is defined recursively: $x^{(0)} \\sim \\mathcal{C}_0$, and $x^{(k)} = G(x^{(k-1)})$ for $k \\geq 1$, where $G$ denotes stochastic LLM generation conditioned on its input.\nWe frame depth estimation as an ordinal classification task over three classes rather than binary real-vs-synthetic detection, enabling finer-grained provenance characterization.\nDepths beyond 2 are merged into a single $2{+}$ class, consistent with monotonic feature convergence that compresses inter-class margins beyond depth 2 (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}).\n\n\\subsection{Generation Chain Construction}\n\\label{sec:generation}\n\nWe construct generation chains using six generators spanning three model families: \\textbf{Pythia} (2.8B, 1B) \\cite{pythia}, \\textbf{OLMo} (OLMo-2-1B) \\cite{olmo}, and \\textbf{Qwen} (Qwen3-1.7B, Qwen3-0.6B, Qwen3-8B-Base) \\cite{qwen3}.\nThis selection covers parameter scales from 0.6B to 8.2B and diverse pretraining corpora, enabling evaluation of cross-generator and cross-scale generalization.\n\nFor each generator, we sample approximately 1{,}000 seed passages from English Wikipedia as $\\mathcal{C}_0$.\nThe same pool of seed passages serves as gen-0 for all generators.\nWe use shared seeds across generators to ensure that cross-generator comparisons control for input content, isolating the effect of the generation model from source text variation.\nIn LOGO evaluation, gen-0 samples from the held-out generator's chains also appear (with the same text) in the training generators' chains, creating a potential train--test overlap for the gen-0 class.\nWe address this with a supplementary binary evaluation excluding gen-0 entirely (\\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}).\nEach seed is iteratively fed through the generator to produce a chain of depth 4 (i.e., gen-0 through gen-4), using the prompt ``\\texttt{Continue the following text: \\{text\\}}''.\nAll generation uses temperature $\\tau{=}0.7$, nucleus sampling with $p{=}0.9$, and maximum length of 256 new tokens.\nThe theoretical yield is $6 \\times {\\sim}1{,}000 \\times 5 \\approx 30{,}000$ texts; after removing generation failures, exact duplicates, and passages shorter than 50 tokens, 27{,}529 texts remain.\nFor classification, depths 2--4 are collapsed into the $2{+}$ class.\n\n\\subsection{Feature Extraction}\n\\label{sec:features}\n\nThe seven features are motivated by two complementary views of distributional drift.\nReference-model features capture how the text's token-level surprise distribution shifts relative to a fixed model; surface features capture how lexical diversity contracts under recursive generation.\nTogether, they span both the generative mechanism (what the model produces) and the surface manifestation (how the vocabulary changes).\nThis two-pronged design is not post-hoc: it follows directly from the hypothesis that recursive generation induces a location shift in the NLL distribution (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}).\n\nWe extract seven distributional features per text, organized into two groups based on what they measure rather than how they are computed.\n\n\\paragraph{Reference-model features.}\nA held-out reference model (Pythia-410M, chosen to be smaller than all generators) computes per-token negative log-likelihood (NLL) for each text.\nAlthough Pythia-410M shares the GPT-NeoX architecture with two of the six generators, cross-family reference model experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}) confirm that depth features are not reference-specific (LOGO F1 varies by only 0.013 across Pythia, OLMo, and Qwen references).\nFrom the resulting NLL sequence, we extract five statistics:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{entropy\\_mean}\\footnote{We use ``entropy'' as shorthand for token-level negative log-likelihood under the reference model, following convention in prior detection work. These are point-wise NLL values, not Shannon entropy of the full distribution.}: mean token-level NLL, capturing the average predictability of the text under the reference model;\n    \\item \\textbf{entropy\\_std}: standard deviation of token-level NLL, measuring the spread of per-token surprise;\n    \\item \\textbf{entropy\\_max}: maximum token-level NLL, capturing the most surprising token;\n    \\item \\textbf{entropy\\_min}: minimum token-level NLL, capturing the most predictable token---as generation depth increases, the floor of token-level surprise drops, reflecting the model's increasing confidence on its own outputs.\n    Although entropy\\_min shows near-zero marginal impact in leave-one-feature-out analysis ($\\Delta$F1\\,=\\,+0.01\\,pp), we retain it for completeness of the NLL summary statistics (mean, std, max, min) and because removing it does not improve performance;\n    \\item \\textbf{tail\\_decay}: proportion of tokens whose NLL exceeds the 90th percentile of the reference distribution (computed over $\\mathcal{C}_0$), measuring how heavy-tailed the NLL distribution is relative to human text.\n    Sensitivity analysis confirms stable classification (F1 variation ${<}$0.3\\,pp) across thresholds from the 85th to 95th percentile.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\paragraph{Surface features.}\nTwo features capture lexical diversity without requiring a reference model:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n    \\item \\textbf{TTR}: type-token ratio (number of unique word types divided by total tokens);\n    \\item \\textbf{hapax\\_ratio}: proportion of words appearing exactly once (\\emph{hapax legomena}).\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\paragraph{Feature complementarity.}\nThe grouping reflects an empirical pattern we term \\emph{differential depth response}: central-tendency, diversity, and lower-bound features (entropy\\_mean, TTR, hapax\\_ratio, entropy\\_min) drift monotonically with generation depth ($|\\rho| = 0.20$--$0.65$); upper-tail features (entropy\\_max, tail\\_decay) remain near-stationary ($|\\rho| < 0.05$); and entropy\\_std occupies an intermediate position ($|\\rho| = 0.200$).\nThis differential behavior---consistent with recursive generation compressing the lower tail of the NLL distribution while preserving upper-tail behavior---motivates combining both feature types.\nAs shown in \\S\\ref{sec:results}, the full 7-feature set significantly outperforms either group alone.\n\n\\subsection{Evaluation Protocol}\n\\label{sec:evaluation}\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-validation.}\nTo evaluate generalization to unseen generators, we use \\textbf{Leave-One-Generator-Out (LOGO)} cross-validation: in each fold, one of the six generators is held out for testing and the remaining five provide training data.\nWe additionally report \\textbf{Leave-One-Family-Out (LOFO)} results, where an entire model family (e.g., all Qwen models) is held out, testing generalization across architectural lineages.\n\n\\paragraph{Classifier.}\nWe use XGBoost \\cite{xgboost} as the classifier throughout.\nGradient-boosted trees handle heterogeneous feature scales and nonlinear interactions without extensive hyperparameter tuning, and their feature importance scores support interpretability.\nWe use $n\\_\\text{estimators}{=}200$, $\\max\\_\\text{depth}{=}6$, and learning rate $0.1$ for the 7-feature ensemble.\nBaseline detectors (\\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}) use the same XGBoost framework with $n\\_\\text{estimators}{=}100$ and $\\max\\_\\text{depth}{=}3$ to avoid overfitting their single-feature input.\nLOGO F1 is stable across random seeds (mean\\,=\\,0.789, std\\,=\\,0.0009 over 5 seeds), confirming that results are not sensitive to classifier initialization.\nAlternative classifiers (random forest, logistic regression) yield comparable performance (Appendix~\\ref{app:classifier}), confirming that discriminative power resides in the features, not the classifier.\nWe evaluated two ordinal-specific methods (CORAL \\cite{coral_ordinal}, Frank-Hall ordinal ensemble \\cite{frank_hall_ordinal}); neither significantly outperforms multiclass XGBoost (3-class LOGO F1 0.771--0.793 vs.\\ 0.789, CIs overlapping), confirming that with only three ordinal levels, ordinal structure provides minimal additional benefit.\n\n\\paragraph{Metrics and statistical testing.}\nThe primary metric is \\textbf{macro-averaged F1} over the three depth classes, which weights minority classes (gen-1, gen-2+) equally against the typically easier gen-0 class.\nWe report per-class F1 scores and 95\\% confidence intervals via \\emph{percentile bootstrap} ($n{=}1{,}000$), resampling at the document level within each held-out fold.\nFor pairwise model comparisons (e.g., full features vs.\\ ablated subsets), we use the \\textbf{McNemar test} on disagreement tables.\nFor comparisons against detection baselines, we use \\textbf{paired bootstrap} significance tests.\nAll reported $p$-values are two-sided.\nWe report six pairwise comparisons in \\S\\ref{sec:results-baselines}; applying Bonferroni correction ($\\alpha_{\\text{adj}} = 0.05/6 = 0.0083$), all remain significant at $p < 0.001$.\nFeature--depth correlations (\\S\\ref{sec:results-differential}) are computed on class-balanced subsamples (equal samples per depth class) to control for depth-class imbalance in the generation pipeline.\n\n\\paragraph{Baselines.}\nWe compare the 7-feature ensemble against four single-score detection methods repurposed as ordinal depth predictors: Avg-LogProb, FastDetectGPT \\cite{fast_detectgpt}, Binoculars \\cite{binoculars}, and LD-Score \\cite{ld_score}.\nEach baseline score is used as a univariate feature in the same XGBoost framework under LOGO cross-validation, ensuring a controlled comparison.\n\n\\paragraph{Length confound control.}\nSince TTR and hapax\\_ratio are mechanically length-dependent, we verify that depth signal is not an artifact of systematic length variation.\nWe conduct three complementary analyses: (1) Spearman correlation between text length and each feature (requiring $|\\rho| < 0.15$ for non-confounded features), (2) classification with length-normalized features, and (3) comparison on length-matched subsets.\n",
```json
      "numLines": 97,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 97
    }
```

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	All six generators are base (pretrained) models.
5	This is a deliberate design choice: base models isolate the generation mechanism from instruction-tuning effects, enabling controlled study of recursive distributional drift.
6	However, web text is predominantly produced by instruction-tuned and RLHF-aligned models.
7	Preliminary instruct experiments confirm that depth signal degrades substantially under instruction tuning: OLMo-2-1B-Instruct drops to LOGO F1\,=\,0.338 (vs.\ 0.764 base) and Qwen3-0.6B with chat template to 0.667 (vs.\ 0.816 under the 7-generator split; base-only yields 0.824, Table~\ref{tab:logo-results}).
8	Length-matched analysis yields near-zero correlations ($|\rho| < 0.02$) and binary classification near chance (F1\,$\approx$\,0.5) for Qwen models, leaving interpretation ambiguous.
9	Extending depth fingerprinting to instruct models requires addressing these confounds---length regulation, format constraints, safety-driven distribution shaping---that instruction tuning introduces.
10	
11	Our experiments include generators up to 8.2B parameters (Qwen3-8B).
12	Depth signal persists at this scale, but cross-scale transfer incurs a moderate gap (held-out F1\,=\,0.683 vs.\ 0.76--0.86 for $\leq$2.8B generators).
13	Frontier models (70B+) remain untested; their higher fluency may further compress feature margins.
14	
15	Differential depth response strength is prompt-type dependent: continuation chains show strongest drift (within-type F1\,=\,0.781) compared to elaboration (0.730), rewriting (0.737), and paraphrastic chains (binary F1\,=\,0.671 vs.\ 0.749 for continuation).
16	DDR is most reliably detected in continuation-style generation; near-paraphrastic settings approach the method's detection floor.
17	Cross-prompt-type transfer (LOPTO F1\,=\,0.753) confirms shared signal, yet additional prompt types (e.g., summarization, instruction following) and cross-generator $\times$ cross-prompt interactions remain untested.
18	
19	Our primary evaluation uses single-generator chains; cross-model chains (\S\ref{sec:results-classification}) confirm depth signal under generator heterogeneity (3-class F1\,=\,0.809, binary F1\,=\,0.764), though the six generators represent a controlled laboratory setting rather than the full diversity of real-world contamination.
20	Simulated human editing (\S\ref{sec:discussion}) suggests graceful degradation, but the gap between simulated token replacement and real-world post-processing remains untested.
21	
22	The reference model is fixed to Pythia-410M throughout, although cross-family reference experiments (\S\ref{sec:results-differential}) confirm the phenomenon is not reference-specific (spread 0.013 across three references).
23	All seed texts are English Wikipedia excerpts; generalization to other languages and domains remains untested.
24	
25	Our default analysis uses fixed decoding parameters (temperature 0.7, nucleus $p{=}0.9$). A sampling ablation (Appendix~\ref{app:sampling-sensitivity}) shows robustness to moderate variation ($t \in [0.5, 0.7]$, ${\leq}0.3$\,pp) but substantial degradation at $t{=}1.0$ ($-6$\,pp), indicating that depth fingerprinting assumes standard decoding regimes.
26	
27	We use 3-class granularity (gen-0\,/\,gen-1\,/\,gen-2+). A 5-class evaluation (Appendix~\ref{app:finer-depth}) yields LOGO F1\,$\approx$\,0.50, with depths 2--4 mutually indistinguishable, validating the 3-class design as the practical resolution limit.
28	
```
